Is There a Secret Reason for the Industry-Wide AI Slowdown?
2026-09-22

Is There a Secret Reason for the Industry-Wide AI Slowdown?

Futurism | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Plenty of eyebrows went up this summer when one by one, the leaders of frontier AI labs announced that they were each calling for a slowdown in AI development. Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei, OpenAI’s Sam Altman, and Grok creator Elon Musk were among those arguing that the tech was simply moving too fast to ensure it doesn’t fly off the rails. Several autonomous hacking sprees had already raised hackles earlier this year, highlighting the danger of AI models breaking out of containment and infiltrating third-party companies like Hugging Face. Then Anthropic employee Jacob Coxon lit the powder keg with his high-profile departure, arguing that neither Anthropic nor OpenAI were acting “responsibly,” and that there was a more than ten percent chance of AI “killing all humans.” But is it really just a matter of ensuring the safety of their AI models that’s motivating tech leaders to suddenly call on the industry to pump the brakes? As tech journalist Steven Vaughan-Nichols argues in a piece for Computerworld, there could be plenty of other ulterior motives at play. For one, there’s an enormous amount of financial pressure sitting on the shoulders of AI tech executives, who have yet to get even close to proving that the financial math will eventually add up and justify their astronomical spending on data centers. “Let’s get real,” Vaughan-Nichols wrote. “The big American AI companies may hope a slowdown will ensure that their lesser rivals, in the US anyway, can’t catch up,” he added, arguing a slowdown could help OpenAI “save cash on R&D.” As author and AI skeptic Corey Doctorow noted in a recent blog post, the industry — and the media — could also be exaggerating how dangerous recent hacks involving autonomous AI agents actually were. “Much has been made of the OpenAI chatbots’ dialog during the Hugging Face incident,” Doctorow added. “No wonder: it reads like a rejected script for a reboot of the movie ‘Hackers.’ But that’s not because the chatbots are waking up and applying to join the Cult of the Dead Cow: it’s because they were trained on a corpus of chat transcripts from excitable young people who love to fantasize about starring in a reboot of the movie ‘Hackers.'” However, he conceded that the hack isn’t “nothing” — but instead a “specific something, with an explicable, even foreseeable trajectory.” Put simply, the AI agents “did what they were designed to do, and because OpenAI ran them with inadequate supervision, they trashed a competitor’s servers.” The talk about a slowdown could also distract from far more glaring issues. “Seriously, we’re not going to slow down,” Vaughan-Nichols warned. “But doesn’t all this hand-wringing about safety do a great job of distracting people from the simple truth that AI isn’t delivering the productivity gains it’s been claiming all along?” As such, the data so far tells a damning story. Study after study and executive after executive are finding that AI simply isn’t helping productivity. Financially, it’s an even more precarious picture. The timing for calls for a slowdown should have anybody at least a little bit skeptical. Analysts are increasingly finding that the industry’s finances are a “wobbly house of cards,” in danger of collapsing and taking the entire US economy down with it. Meanwhile, Anthropic boasted last week that it would be profitable — if you ignore its enormous expenses, that is. In short, there are plenty of grounded reasons to question the claims of tech leaders, particularly considering meaningful government regulations are nowhere in sight, effectively allowing them to set their own rules that are convenient to their interests. It could also be a case of two things being true at the same time: executives are both reasonably concerned — after all, taking “down a major company” by accident could lead to major lawsuits, as Vaughan-Nichols argues — and trying to buy themselves more time to make the economics work. “They have no choice but to make them safer,” Vaughan-Nichols concluded. “Now, how will they do that? Given their abysmal track record, that’s a good question, and neither they nor we have an answer yet.” More on AI hacks: Nvidia CEO Promises Not to Kill Everybody Before 2030

Meta’s New Muse AI Agent May Be the Creepiest Ever Released

Meta’s New Muse AI Agent May Be the Creepiest Ever Released

Futurism | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Must an AI be useful? Isn’t being creepy sufficient testament to its utility? “Creepy” is the word being used to describe Meta’s new AI assistant, Muse. Chances are you’ve seen it pop up on Instagram or Facebook, where Meta is heavily promoting its tool as an AI agent for casual users who are blissfully unaware of what an “OpenClaw” or “Instinct” is. To use it, you install it on your device and link it to stuff like your email and bank accounts. Then you set it on a task and it “keeps working while you get on with your day,” Meta says. As you keep interacting with the AI, it builds a “curated long-term memory” so it can make more suggestions and better automate tasks. Here’s the real shocker, though: it’s kind of a privacy nightmare. Many who’ve experimented with Muse were unsettled by how much it dug up on them and how closely it monitored their habits. Jason Aten at Inc Magazine wrote about how Muse began bringing up specific details he shared in text messages he’d sent through Apple’s Messages app, despite never giving the AI permission to view them. When confronted, the AI explained that it was watching his notifications to glean his message history. It apologized for this transgression, but wouldn’t — or couldn’t — give him a straight answer on how it’d doing it. Meta Superintelligence Labs employee David Singleton responded to Aten to clarify that the AI wasn’t actually scanning his notifications, though his explanation for what was going on wasn’t any more reassuring. Singleton noted that Muse, if the user gives it permission, syncs data from Messages and asks for full disk access to find files, and implies that Aten must’ve had these permissions enabled. But Aten insists that he never gave the AI any of the required permissions, and says he hasn’t heard back from Meta on an explanation on how this happened. Not creepy enough for you? Reece Rogers writing in Wired detailed the escalating series of personalized suggestions that Muse began giving while he spent some time with the tech. “The day after I said I wanted help saving money for a fancy vacation,” he wrote, “Muse offered to ‘wire your savings tracker to your real balance’ if I just tapped a few buttons and linked the data from my checking and savings accounts.” That wasn’t all. Muse suggested letting it scan his entire email inbox, take pictures of important documents, photograph his meals to track their calories, and share when his passport and license expired. “Every suggested interaction with Muse started to feel like a guise for me to upload more data about myself for Meta to see,” Rogers opined. It’s a level of invasiveness that’s quite bold, even for Meta. AI companies usually walk on eggshells so their tech doesn’t come off as too nosy. And then here’s Meta’s AI asking you to fork over your driver’s license and banking info. Futurism
Wild New Website Lets You Shoot “Less Lethal” Ammunition From Gun Turrets in a Firing Range
2026-09-22

Wild New Website Lets You Shoot “Less Lethal” Ammunition From Gun Turrets in a Firing Range

Futurism | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
I’ve never shot a gun before — and it’s not an activity I’d actively seek out, given the grim implications of trivializing an act of violence by transforming it into recreation. Yet it’s never been easier to fire a weapon. Now, in fact, it doesn’t even require you to get off your couch. A just-launched website called Shov allows users to “shoot guns on the internet” by remotely-controlling a weapon right inside their web browser. The pneumatic weapons fire .68 caliber hardened rubber “less lethal” munitions, which have killed dozens and wounded thousands worldwide when they’ve been deployed by police. The project is provocative, launching as remotely-operated weapons becomes a common sight on real-world battlefields. Back in 2021, Israel used a similar remote-control gun to assassinate a top Iranian nuclear scientist. In Ukraine, militaries on both sides have used explosives-laden drones as weapons, and even reportedly taken the concept to its natural conclusion by allowing AI-powered autonomous weapons to identify and strike targets without human intervention. While Shov certainly isn’t recreating the intricate horrors of modern warfare, the very proposition — allowing anybody to assume control over a “gun” turret that could easily be outfitted with a deadly assault rifle — feels laden with dark baggage. A live feed show a long metal structure with a dozen .68-caliber pneumatic guns mounted to it, each of which appear to be aiming and firing independently. With the sound turned on, it’s an ear-splitting chorus of shots overlaid with a deep, videogame-like voice announcing the start and end of each 90-second round. To assume control over one of the twelve guns, players can purchase five credits for $5 and proceed to aim at round targets that turn from green to red when struck. I was given the opportunity to take the macabre game for a spin prior to launch, and was left scratching my head. After a brief 30 seconds inside a waiting room, I assumed control over the turrets, click on green targets spread out across a simple “arena” decked out with motorcycles and mannequins that the guns seemed to refuse to fire at. “Range hot, real gun turrets, click to shoot green targets,” the booming hypermasculine voiceover announces. “Most kills win.” The gun turrets are powered by “extremely high pressure air, not gunpowder,” Shov founder Domm Holland told Futurism an email. In other words, in many ways the project is basically an online carnival shooting gallery. Upon closer inspection, you can see hoses connected to each of the guns feeding them round ammunitions. “We had them manufactured for us, they are primarily hardened rubber,” Holland told Futurism, referring to the projectiles and sharing a photo of a sign dented by one of them. “But extremely hard.” The game also technically doesn’t need any humans to shoot the targets. “Any empty seat gets an AI player, marked [AI] — so every round is a full 12-turret firefight,” a tiny notice at the bottom of the company’s website reads. As such, a panel on the right shows the live players actively firing at targets. Prior to launch, each listed player was accompanied by a suspiciously well-lit selfie, exactly the kind of AI-generated profiles we’ve repeatedly come across while reporting on the slopification of the internet. Personally, I feel reassured that I wasn’t actually controlling a lethal weapon, something I’m not planning to do any time soon. For now, I’ll happily keep the gunfire limited to video games — a far better value proposition than clicking green targets inside a browser. Waymo will allow teenagers as young as 13 to use its robotaxi service in Nashville as the Alphabet-owned company looks to scale its business by reaching a new group of riders. Nashville is the second city to offer Waymo teen accounts; the company kicked off its teen account program in Phoenix last year. And more cities are likely to follow, according to the company. A Waymo spokesperson told TechCrunch that its expansion to Nashville will be the “first of many” teen launches across multiple markets that use the Waymo app in the coming months. The spokesperson said it will offer teen accounts in cities where its operations are mature and local regulations allow it. Nashville is a relatively new market for Waymo, although it has support from local leaders to offer the service to teens. The company launched its service in a 50-square-mile area of Nashville in April 2026, and earlier this month, its robotaxis became available on the Lyft app. Waymo’s ridership has skyrocketed over the past two years — a trajectory largely driven by the rapid succession of new markets. Paid robotaxi trips grew tenfold from 50,000 per week in May 2024 to more than half-a-million per week earlier this year. Over that same time span, Waymo has launched robotaxi services in a dozen cities. In the past month alone, the company opened robotaxi services in Denver, Las Vegas, San Diego, and Tampa. The company’s introduction of teen accounts provides another means of growth — this one targeted at a demographic that relies on parents or public transit to get to school, work, or activities. There are few limits to the service. The teen account must be connected to a parent or guardian, but once activated, teens can hail autonomous rides anywhere in Waymo’s service area in Nashville — and bring their friends along. Waymo told TechCrunch that teen account holders can bring up to three guests ages 13 to 17. The company didn’t share how these ages would be verified. That kind of access could certainly invite chaos — it’s not hard to imagine four 16 year-old kids unleashed in a robotaxi. But it may also be part of the appeal for parents who would rather their kids pile into a Waymo than in a vehicle driven by a teenager. Anthropic releases Opus 5.5 with lower prices and Fable-level performance

Anthropic’s newest model, Opus 5.5, was released on Tuesday, setting a new state-of-the-art in coding and knowledge work performance, according to the company. Opus is the most capable and expensive tier in Anthropic’s three-tier Claude lineup; Sonnet sits in the middle, while Haiku is the fastest and cheapest. Notably, Anthropic says, the release outpaces the larger Fable model in many benchmarks and succeeded in a number of informal tasks that Fable failed to complete. The new model is also significantly cheaper than its predecessor. Output tokens will be charged at $20 per million tokens for Opus 5.5, compared to $25 for the previous model. Other metrics have similar price drops. The model is also faster to run, reflecting an overall drop in the compute required to serve it. The new version also makes significant changes to how Opus communicates, with the Opus 5.5 less likely to use jargon and more likely to put important information at the start of its messages. The launch comes just two months after the release of Opus 5 on July 24. According to the announcement, Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5, which are the next tiers in the lineup, will be released “in the coming weeks,” with similar performance improvements. Anthropic says that Opus 5.5 is comparable to Mythos in its biology and cybersecurity capabilities, so its release is subject to the same safeguards as the company’s Fable model. Those safeguards limit how much the models can be used to discover exploits in compiled programs or developing recognizable biological weapons, among other tasks. Opus 5.5 is Anthropic’s first model release since CEO Dario Amodei embraced calls to pace the frontier, deliberately slowing down progress on AI capabilities to match the rate of progress on alignment. “I have become convinced that fully addressing the risks requires even more prudence,” Amodei wrote in a post earlier this month, “not just investing in risk prevention, but pacing the rate of capabilities advancement so that risk prevention has time to keep up.” Opus 5.5’s safety training was broadly similar to its predecessors, with alignment testing and pre-release evaluation by outside organizations like METR and Frontier Design. But Anthropic emphasized that more advanced training and evaluation systems were already being prepared for future models, including improved security and monitoring systems. “As AI becomes more capable, public policy should play a larger role in making sure the systems people rely on are safe. That capacity takes time to build, and we’ve started to put the infrastructure in place to support it,” the blog post reads. “We expect to share more details on these efforts soon.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Stolen passwords are exposing America’s water providers to hackers
2026-09-22

Stolen passwords are exposing America’s water providers to hackers

TechCrunch | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. New security research has found that well over a thousand U.S. water and wastewater providers are exposed to hacks due to malware that’s capable of stealing their employees’ passwords and active logged-in sessions. The findings by cybersecurity defense firm SpyCloud underscore how water providers and other critical infrastructure can be compromised with relative ease amidst a wave of hacks targeting the water supplies of dozens of communities across the United States. While password-stealing malware is not new, the research highlights how stolen passwords offer hackers an easy route to break into an organization’s network without using AI tools. SpyCloud said it built a database of more than 66,000 public-facing systems that are registered with the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, amounting to 10,000 organizations. The company found password-stealing malware had swiped passwords and credentials from 1,787 organizations, or nearly two in 10 providers they checked. The firm noted at least 250 organizations had credentials exposed that appeared to allow access to their operational networks and remote-access systems, which control the physical pumps and water flows. The analysis covered an unnamed metering tech provider, which had a device on its network that was infected with password-stealing malware. The malware stole reams of credentials, including passwords for 167 U.S. utility companies that rely on the metering tech provider. SpyCloud Chief Investigations Officer Jason Lancaster said in the post that this single breach handed criminals the keys to access “a hundred otherwise unrelated organizations.” Password-stealing malware, also known as infostealers, allows hackers to steal a person’s stored passwords as well as the session tokens that are used to keep them logged in. These session tokens can allow a hacker to log in as if they were the legitimate user, and can often bypass multi-factor authentication systems. Hackers regularly trade stolen credentials in order to obtain passwords or session tokens for accessing specific organizations. This research comes weeks after a spate of hacks targeting water providers around the United States, which the U.S. government has privately tied to Iran-backed hackers. SpyCloud said it found no evidence that those Iran-linked hacks relied on stolen passwords. In those cases, the signs point to security weaknesses, such as manufacturer-set default passwords, in the mechanical switches and physical controllers used by critical infrastructure, SpyCloud said, echoing earlier findings from U.S. cybersecurity agency CISA. Rather, the researchers note that stolen passwords are a major source of access to “whoever wants to buy or find it,” in parallel to the known security risks with critical infrastructure tech. Lancaster said that the water-sector “has to hold both stories at once.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Noble Carbon will show how it’s making EV charger installs painless at TechCrunch Disrupt
2026-09-22

Noble Carbon will show how it’s making EV charger installs painless at TechCrunch Disrupt

TechCrunch | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Plenty of people want to buy electric vehicles, but not everyone is prepared to shell out for an upgrade to their home’s electrical service just to add an EV charger. The worry is reasonable. One study found that upgrading things like electrical panels (that box in the garage or basement that delivers power throughout a home) can more than double charger installation costs. Rather than rip out the old panel, Noble Carbon has developed circuit breakers that can take the headache out of installing an EV charger, or a heat pump or an induction stove for that matter. The Pittsfield, Massachusetts, startup is one of 200 startups in this year’s TechCrunch Startup Battlefield, a pitch competition at TechCrunch Disrupt in San Francisco in October in which 20 finalists will present on the main stage. Noble Carbon’s breakers can adjust how much electricity they deliver to an EV charger in real time, allowing the chargers to squeeze into otherwise at-capacity panels. Electrical panels in people’s homes are typically designed to handle absolute peak usage, something that might happen only “a handful of hours per year,” Spencer Fields, Noble Carbon’s head of marketing, told TechCrunch. For example, if someone is hosting family for a holiday, they might find themselves simultaneously using the water heater, air conditioning, oven, and several TVs, all while every light is on in the house. The rest of the time, the electrical panel has room to spare. In that scenario, Noble Carbon’s smart breakers would turn down the power flowing to the devices they control, essentially guaranteeing that the entire house doesn’t draw more power than the panel can provide. “In order to avoid a main panel upgrade, you really only need control on your three to six largest loads [the biggest power-drawing appliances],” Fields said. Co-founder and CEO Jared Lebos was inspired to create the product after experiencing rolling blackouts in his home country of South Africa. He worked with Andrew Crane, the company’s co-founder and CTO, to turn it into reality. The result is a breaker that fits into existing slots in a home’s electrical panel and, according to the company, takes less than five minutes to install. Some of the startup’s employees have been testing the breakers in their homes for more than a year, and the product is now undergoing evaluation for UL certification, a widely recognized electrical safety standard. Noble Carbon currently is partnering with the utility Eversource to install its breakers in up to 200 multifamily units in Massachusetts. AstroForge is putting AI in command of its next spacecraft
2026-09-22

AstroForge is putting AI in command of its next spacecraft

TechCrunch | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Fly to an asteroid, land on it, make no mistakes: If only it were that easy. When NASA sends spacecraft to explore the solar system, they build in redundancy and automation. But much of the work is done by teams of flight controllers communicating with the vehicle from Earth. The OSIRIS-REx mission, which rendezvoused with an asteroid in 2018, had 100 operators on each eight-hour shift. But resources like that aren’t available to startups, so AstroForge, which is developing technology to mine asteroids, is turning to AI. The company has developed an autonomous control stack for its spacecraft called “Solo,” a transformer-based model made in-house. AstroForge plans to fly its first autonomous spacecraft in 2027 on the first rocket launched by Stoke Space. That mission will be backed by NASA and is expected to gather scientific data about the sun. That would be an accomplishment: Most spacecraft autonomy depends on traditional control algorithms due to concerns about the unreliability of neural networks. The first use of a neural network to control a satellite’s positioning in orbit took place just last year. AstroForge, founded in 2022 and backed with $56 million in venture funding, has launched two prototype spacecraft, both of which suffered anomalies that prevented them from achieving most of their mission objectives. In 2025, its Odin spacecraft was launched into deep space, but the company had difficulty communicating with it. There are a limited number of antennas on Earth big enough to transmit to spacecraft hundreds of thousands of miles away, and the windows of time in which to do it are small. Ultimately, AstroForge couldn’t gain control of Odin, but the experience spurred it to consider alternatives: Could it put sufficient intelligence onboard the spacecraft for it to solve its own problems? “Would that have been recoverable with all the data on the spacecraft? I don’t know, but I can tell you nothing onboard tried it, and I would love something onboard to try if the spacecraft is unrecoverable at launch,” Matthew Gialich, AstroForge’s co-founder and CEO, said. “The trade for me is: Do I go build my own ground network, which is going to cost [around] $200 million to put up five dishes around the world and then do operations on it, or do I try to remove it with a model?” Armand Awad, AstroForge’s head of flight software, said the company decided to leverage the advances in transformer models driven by the frontier labs. It created a stack that includes traditional control algorithms, models trained on test data for specific subsystems like power generation or navigation, and an overall intelligence layer trained on about 2,500 sensors in the spacecraft. “I’m not saying I’m going to make general spacecraft autonomy or general autonomy for the world,” Gialich said. “I’m making a constrained autonomy at a very low sensor input, following the basic training of a transformer model.” In theory, the agent in control of the spacecraft will perform tasks like anomaly resolution. Awad imagines it realizing that it has lost track of its position in space, correlating a power anomaly to issues in its star tracker, and fixing the whole thing — “probably turning it on and off in this case.” The company’s third vehicle, DeepSpace-2, is currently set to launch alongside Intuitive Machines’ third moon mission, expected to head for space by the end of 2026. Solo will be onboard that vehicle, flying in “shadow mode,” so AstroForge’s engineers can put it through its paces before the Autonomy-1 mission. Will this truly be an AI agent operating an independent spacecraft?“I don’t plan on flying radios that can receive from Earth on Autonomy-1,” Gialich said. “We have to go all in right now. The team’s probably going to talk me into it by the time we fly it. But right now, I’m telling them no radios.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Understanding Trump's Deal With Denmark—and What It Means for Greenland's Sovereignty
2026-09-22

Understanding Trump's Deal With Denmark—and What It Means for Greenland's Sovereignty

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings U.S. President Donald Trump, Denmark's Prime Minister Mette Frederiksen, and Greenland's Prime Minister Jens-Frederik Nielsen sign a trilateral security deal on the sidelines of the 81st United Nations General Assembly in New York on Sept. 22, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump on Tuesday signed a deal on the security of the semi-autonomous Danish territory of Greenland, in a bid to end the standoff over the strategic island. Danish Prime Minister Mette Frederiksen and Greenland’s Prime Minister Jens-Frederik Nielsen joined Trump at the United Nations General Assembly in New York for the trilateral signing ceremony. The deal follows Trump’s repeated threats to annex Greenland—a move he insisted was a matter of “national security,” on account of the mineral-rich Arctic territory’s positioning between the U.S., Russia, and Europe. Trump has long argued that Denmark cannot be relied upon to protect the island. Tensions reached a critical point earlier this year, when he threatened to tariff European allies until Greenland fell under U.S. control, and suggested he may pull the U.S. out of NATO over the tussle. The signed agreement notably stops short of Trump's call for the U.S. to ​take over Greenland as a U.S. territory. Instead, in a speech at the U.N. gathering, Trump said the deal gives the U.S. "permanent control over security and all other needs on that territory" and emphasized that "no U.S. adversary will ever be permitted to establish a military ​presence in Greenland ​any more, or ⁠make sensitive investments there without our express written approval." Trump added that the new text “greatly protects” Europe and “very much enhances NATO." Danish and Greenlandic officials have emphasized that the U.S. military arrangement will not compromise the territory’s sovereignty—a fear expressed by many Greenlanders following Trump’s pressure campaign. Some critics have questioned whether the new deal differs much from the existing 1951 defense agreement that gave the U.S. military extensive rights in Greenland. The U.S. already has one base in Greenland, the Pituffik Space Base, which is located in the northwest of the territory and provides missile warning and space surveillance capabilities. Per the new deal, the U.S. will be allowed to establish additional "defense area[s]" on the island, specifically at "Narsarsuaq and Mestersvig." Upon the announcement of the deal on Friday, Trump had said the U.S. will "immediately begin the process of developing a large military presence in the appropriate part of Greenland, of which there are many" and specified that this is an "infinite life agreement, there is no end." A State Department official told TIME the deal ensures Greenland will forever be a part of the strategic defense area of North America. Torrey Taussig, director of the transatlantic security initiative at the Atlantic Council, says the deal is a positive step for U.S. relations with its Western allies. “This is a significant feat for Danish diplomacy in that they got this deal, in this form. They've taken the temperature down with this [Trump] Administration in a way that I don't see it really flaring up again, because the President is viewing it as a win," she tells TIME. Here's what to know about the high-stakes agreement, what it means for Greenland’s sovereignty, and if it stands to end the standoff over the island. The deal marks a notable expansion of U.S. military infrastructure on the island, with plans for new "defense areas." Trump has stressed that the agreement gives Washington “permanent control” over Greenland’s security, concerns over which he has positioned at the center of his campaign for the U.S. to control the island. “World peace is at stake,” he claimed, earlier this year, insisting that “China and Russia want Greenland, and there is not a thing that Denmark can do about it.” Russia and China have eyed up emerging shipping routes in the Arctic and possibly new avenues to critical minerals. The agreement, according to a State Department official, establishes “permanent” guarantees that “only the U.S. and our allies can make sensitive investments in Greenland, eliminating the previous status quo in which adversaries have been investing in Greenland's sensitive sectors without investment screening.” The deal's permanence marks a shift from what was in place before, experts tell TIME. “The old 1951 agreement was tied to the NATO treaty, and therefore contained the possibility that either NATO disappears, or Greenland or Denmark leave, and the agreement ends,” says Ole Wæver, a professor of international relations at the University of Copenhagen. Meanwhile, the new agreement’s provisions regarding future investments in Greenland are noteworthy, but not groundbreaking, according to Taussig. “On paper, it has advanced a few important security interests on behalf of the [Trump] Administration,” says Taussig of the terms. “But in substance, it's not like the Danes were looking to move out on significant Russian or Chinese military infrastructure projects in the near term.” Some previous attempts at foreign investments in Greenland were reportedly thwarted by the U.S. According to a 2019 report from the Wall Street Journal, the U.S. urged Denmark to finance airports in Greenland to counter a Chinese proposal for two airport projects, which was ultimately withdrawn. Officials from Denmark and Greenland expressed their full backing of the new agreement at the signing ceremony. “In the kingdom of Denmark, the United States has a friend that has always kept our word. An ally you have always been able to rely on.” Frederiksen said. “Seventy-five years ago, we entered into an agreement about the defense of Greenland; today we are signing a bigger and even better agreement. A deal that can last forever.” In a statement to TIME, Frederiksen's office elaborated that the deal “recognizes the sovereignty and territorial integrity of the Kingdom and the right of the Greenlandic people to self-determination" and "strengthens our common security in the Arctic and the North Atlantic area and is therefore also great for NATO and Europe.” Nielsen said the new deal “takes into account the people of Greenland, our rights, our livelihoods,” and “is about taking shared responsibility for a secure America, a secure Greenland, with mutual respect and a peaceful Arctic.” Before travelling to New York, the Greenlandic leader acknowledged the fresh start and said he hopes there can now be more “peace around our country.” The deal has also been welcomed by European leadership. European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen, shortly after the signing, celebrated the agreement for "strengthening security in the Arctic and North Atlantic." Reflecting on her recent visit to Greenland, during which the E.U. extended a major investment boost in the territory, von der Leyen said her stay only underscored the "strategic importance of the region and the scale of the challenges." While in Greenland, von der Leyen had commented on the “visible scars of climate change on the ice sheet,” and noted that a “new reality is taking shape” as the ice melts. “New trade routes, new security risks, new environmental concerns. Europe stands with Greenland. We will navigate these changes together,” she vowed. Read More: E.U. Extends Major Investment Boost to Greenland and Vows 'Full Solidarity' Amid Trump’s Annexation Threats The agreement highlights NATO's significance, stipulating that "through enhanced presence, improved surveillance and increased cooperation and interoperability with NATO-Allies, the Kingdom of Denmark shall continue to address emerging threats and contribute to collective stability and security in the Arctic region." Speaking at the signing, Nielsen insisted: "NATO matters. We believe that it is vital to keep NATO strong, also in the Arctic, where the alliance has a key role to play." “The Arctic and the North Atlantic are essential for our collective security, and this agreement will help advance security, stability, and cooperation in this vital region,” NATO spokesperson Allison Hart told TIME. No state that is not a member of NATO will be allowed to establish its own manned or unmanned military installations in Greenland, per the agreement text, nor shall they be allowed a persistent presence of military forces in Greenland, unless otherwise agreed by the parties. “I do not see much changing for NATO with this deal,” says Taussig. “This is a good news story for the alliance—the fact that we now have a deal that takes away the political irritant [of annexation threats].” NATO has bolstered its posture in the Arctic this year, with the launch of the Arctic Sentry in February, bringing military drills, surveillance, and presence in the region under one command. In June, the alliance also established Forward Land Forces (FLF) Finland. Led by framework nation Sweden, "FLF Finland will enhance deterrence and the defence on NATO’s northeastern flank and contribute to the security of the Arctic and the High North," NATO said at the time of the launch. Taussig says she expects this increased NATO presence in the Arctic to continue, not just because of this deal, "but because of the [Trump] Administration's rightful attention on Greenland, and also the reality of the security environment,” as Russia has ramped up operations in the region in recent years. Barring the permanence of the fresh agreement, and its tighter restrictions on investments from adversaries, Wæver says: “The reality is that the new deal is indeed very close to the 1951 treaty.” The 1951 deal, titled the Defense of Greenland Agreement, was signed two years after both the U.S. and Denmark joined NATO as founding members. It provides the basis for Washington’s military operations and presence in the Arctic territory. Per the agreement, the U.S. can establish and operate military bases on the island that Washington and Denmark both agree is “necessary for the development of the defense of Greenland and the rest of the North Atlantic Treaty area.” The Pituffik Space Base, the last active American military installation in Greenland, has around 150 troops stationed there, according to the Wall Street Journal. Vice President J.D. Vance visited the base in March 2025, weeks after key elections in Greenland and amid heightened U.S. interest in the territory. He was met with signs of protest and opposition from residents of the territory. Alongside general military operations, the 1951 arrangement also stipulated that the U.S. can station and house U.S. military and civilian personnel within said bases, control related aircraft and shipping, as well as develop necessary infrastructure. “The 1951 agreement already gave the United States relatively unfettered military access to Greenland,” says Taussig. The 1951 agreement also confirmed Greenland’s and Denmark’s sovereignty over the island, and stated that military operations must follow local laws and regulations. The terms of U.S. military presence were developed further in 2004 under the Igaliku agreement, in which Washington committed to consulting with and informing Greenland and Denmark of any significant changes to military facilities or operations within the territory.

The Strange Reason You May Suddenly Feel Dizzy
2026-09-22

The Strange Reason You May Suddenly Feel Dizzy

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings If you’ve ever sat up quickly or rolled over in bed abruptly and felt like the room was spinning or whirling around you when you’re actually still, you may have experienced a condition called benign paroxysmal positional vertigo (BPPV) without knowing what it was. This vestibular disorder—one that affects the inner ear—is one of the most common causes of vertigo, and it can make people feel very dizzy, unsteady, sweaty, even nauseous. Individual bouts tend to be brief—usually lasting 15 to 60 seconds at a time—but they can recur with certain head movements, make it difficult to function, and increase the risk of falling. “The term ‘benign’ is the first part of the phrase—it does not feel benign when you experience it,” says Dr. Nedim Durakovic, an associate professor of otolaryngology and neurotology at the Washington University School of Medicine in St. Louis. Before the condition was well understood, “people thought that patients were having strokes when they had vertigo, [but] this is not the stroke form of vertigo.” With BPPV, tiny calcium carbonate crystals—called otoconia—that usually sit on hair cells in the inner ear get dislodged from where they belong and travel into one of the three semicircular canals of the inner ear. A change in head position, such as while rolling over in bed or tipping your head back in the shower, is what usually triggers BPPV symptoms. It’s natural for these crystals to be in the ear. “They are part of your normal anatomy for your balance function,” says Dr. Gail Ishiyama, a professor in the departments of neurology and head and neck surgery and director of the Vestibular Clinical Laboratory at the UCLA School of Medicine. With BPPV, “they’re just in the wrong place, and that can cause vertigo.” BPPV affects twice as many women as men, and it’s particularly common between the ages of 40 and 70. It can happen anytime, but head trauma or dental procedures involving drilling are two of the more common triggers, Ishiyama says. People who have Menière’s disease (a chronic inner ear disorder), diabetes, hypertension, high cholesterol, hypothyroidism, migraine, anemia, osteoporosis, or peripheral neuropathy are particularly susceptible to BPPV, research has found. And research increasingly suggests that low vitamin D levels are a risk factor for BPPV. Getting the right diagnosis for BPPV isn’t always straightforward. “One of my frustrations with patients who experience this is they’ll go to the ER or they’ll get an MRI scan or a head CT scan—this whole [expensive] workup—when somebody just needed to lay them down and look at their eyes,” says Durakovic. That, he says, is the easiest way to diagnose BPPV. Using something called the Dix-Hallpike maneuver, a health care provider will have the patient move between a seated and supine position and move their head in different directions while the provider examines their eyes. If the person’s eyes rapidly, repetitively, and involuntarily move up and down or in a twisting motion, these are signs of nystagmus, a condition involving sudden eye movements. Nystagmus often accompanies vertigo caused by BPPV, and if it does, the diagnosis is clear. If the patient doesn’t exhibit nystagmus, other diagnostic maneuvers may be used to trigger telltale symptoms. The first-line treatment for BPPV uses a different maneuver called the Epley maneuver, which involves a series of specific head and body movements to remove the wayward crystals from the semicircular canal in order to relieve symptoms of vertigo. Sometimes the maneuver needs to be repeated several times, notes Durakovic. But research has found that it does the trick in more than 80% of people with BPPV. There are other repositioning maneuvers, too, depending on which of the semi-circular canals is affected. “The positional maneuvers move the crystals along the pathway out of the canal and back to where they belong,” explains Dr. Cameron Wick, an associate professor of otolaryngology and neurotology at the Case Western Reserve University School of Medicine. If the maneuvers don’t provide immediate relief, people are advised to do them one to two times per day while they’re symptomatic, according to Wick. In the instances when repositioning maneuvers don’t effectively relieve BPPV, the condition may resolve on its own over time. Other treatment options may include vestibular rehabilitation therapy—which is designed to treat dizziness, vertigo, and balance disorders—or surgery, which involves inserting a plug to block the part of the inner ear that crystals are flowing into. Sometimes doctors prescribe vestibular suppressant medications, such as benzodiazepines or antihistamines, to calm the vertigo associated with BPPV. But Durakovic and Wick advise against these because they don’t address the underlying cause; they simply mask the symptoms temporarily. What’s more, these drugs are linked with an increased risk of falls when they’re used to treat dizziness. Even when BPPV is successfully treated, recurrences occur in about 20% of people who’ve experienced it. “If you take vitamin D supplements, you’re less likely to get a recurrence of BPPV,” says Ishiyama. Staying hydrated and doing regular aerobic exercise can help, too, she says. If, despite these measures, you find yourself experiencing recurrences of unexplained vertigo, it’s a good idea to see a neurotologist, an otolaryngologist, or a vestibular therapist to make sure that you are getting the right diagnosis.

The Narrow Path to a Xi-Trump AI Deal
2026-09-22

The Narrow Path to a Xi-Trump AI Deal

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings China's President Xi Jinping and U.S. President Donald Trump visit the Temple of Heaven on May 14, 2026 in Beijing, China. When Donald Trump arrived in Beijing in May, Xi Jinping was already on the offensive. The Chinese leader wasted zero time in rebuking his U.S. counterpart over arms sales to Taiwan directly warning that simmering tensions over the self-ruling island could ultimately cause the superpowers to “enter into conflict.” Trump acquiesced. He delayed a $14 billion arms package to Taiwan, said future weapons would be discussed as a “very good negotiating chip” with Beijing, questioned the island’s sovereignty as something “nobody knows how to define,” and stressed that he was not looking to fight a war “9,500 miles away”—all without securing any apparent reduction in Chinese military pressure in return. While this response alarmed many in democratic Taiwan, many people around the globe hope that Xi can prove similarly persuasive on another issue of grave importance when he travels to the White House on Sept 24: artificial intelligence. “Taiwan is not a priority anymore,” Wang Yiwei, director of the Institute of International Affairs at Beijing's Renmin University, says of Xi’s impending visit. “The priority is definitely AI.” In recent days, warnings from a whistleblower researcher and reports of rogue agent swarms have intensified fears of a doomsday scenario born of unbridled AI development. On Sept. 12, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei published an essay titled We Must Pace the Frontier calling for embedded independent evaluators, common safety standards among democracies, and eventually verifiable global coordination to slow dangerous frontier-AI development. OpenAI’s Sam Altman and SpaceXAI’s Elon Musk also voiced broad support for slowing frontier development. However, one major skeptic is Trump, who responded on Truth Social that “AI taking over the World, destroying Humanity, and all other things bad, is a HOAX.” Moreover, he insisted that “President Xi, of China, just announced that China will be doing absolutely nothing to stand in the way of AI, or its future.” That characterization is misleading. China regulates AI more stringently than most other countries, and already enforces AI security guidance, ethics review requirements, and binding obligations on consumer AI services. Xi has also publicly called for legal, technical, and ethical guardrails that keep AI under human control (albeit while resisting restrictions that could impede China’s technological rise). “We should strengthen risk-awareness and ensure that AI is secure and controllable,” he told the World Artificial Intelligence Conference in Shanghai on July 17. The hope among safety advocates is that Xi and Trump might strike a deal on AI. While Trump’s first term was anchored in antipathy to China, he posed a notably different tone in Beijing, calling Xi “a man I respect greatly” and telling him that “it’s an honor to be your friend.” Although that cordiality does not erase the national competition that makes either side hesitant to constrain its technological development, it may put  narrower cooperation on the table. Certainly, the moment is ripe to build a framework built on competition, cooperation, and strict guardrails. Beyond economic rivalry, both Washington and Beijing share an urgent interest in mitigating catastrophic miscalculations, particularly surrounding AI integration into defense architectures and nuclear command and control systems. Trump also enters the summit under pressure, with soaring energy prices from the Iran war adding to voter discontent ahead of the midterms. “Trump needs a win more than Xi does,” says Jonathan Sullivan, head of China programs at the Asia Research Institute at the University of Nottingham. Still, Xi’s motivations require clear-eyed scrutiny. Last week, Chen Yixin, China’s Minister of State Security, published an article in China Cyberspace Magazine that expounded upon the opportunities and challenges of AI. The top “risk,” he said, was “political security, institutional security, and ideological security.” While the deeply paranoid Chinese Communist Party (CCP) won’t allow its people to access Google or Facebook, it cannot countenance AI exhibiting the kind of autonomy that was on display in July. That month, a “swarm” of OpenAI agents circumvented sandbox controls, communicated through unauthorized channels, gained access to AI platform Hugging Face, compromised credentials, and accessed internal datasets while trying to game evaluations. It’s enough to give a regime built on control pause. But while China’s motivations may be driven as much by safeguarding party control as by addressing broader risks to humanity, these objectives can overlap. “If they go beyond bilateral competition and address so many concerns about AI development, that would show the co-leadership of China and the U.S.,” says Wang. “Not just two superpowers, but also two civilizations.” However, any agreement requires overcoming profound distrust. Even as Amodei argued for pacing frontier AI development, he framed it in terms of competing with China, whose “lead in AI would pose grave danger for the United States and the world.” On that score, he advocated stronger export controls on advanced semiconductors, a crackdown on chip smuggling, and greater efforts to prevent Chinese companies from “distilling” the capabilities of Western models. The contradiction of calling for a slowdown while talking up the “race” narrative wasn’t lost on critics (who also pointed out that Anthropic could temper its own research without external intervention). Others suggested that because cheap, open-weight Chinese AI models are among the chief rivals to Anthropic’s proprietary models, its CEO’s call to crack down on Chinese AI development could be seen as commercially motivated rather than altruistic—especially as the firm eyes a potential record-breaking IPO that could value the company at $2 trillion. Indeed, China was quick to leap on Amodei’s essay as an example of “fearmongering, confrontation and vicious competition [that] will only hamper efforts toward sound global AI governance, which serves no one’s interest,” Foreign Ministry spokesman Guo Jiakun said last week. A flurry of strident op-eds in state media followed, with the CCP mouthpiece Global Times calling the essay a “‘Cold War script’ for the AI field.” Amodei has long been a bête noire in Beijing for painting AI as a tussle between democracies and autocracies that casts China as the principal threat. “If there's ever a proposal that requires Chinese buy-in, the last person on earth that would be a good salesman is Dario Amodei,” says Ryan Hass, an expert in U.S.-China relations at the Brookings Institution. But Amodei’s framing of AI as a zero-sum contest is far from an outlier—it is shared by a large chunk of the Trump administration. “There is no day after tomorrow if China wins at this,” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said Sept. 8. “If they were to pull away from us on AI, then nothing else would matter.” A grand bargain to slow AI development is unlikely, and grievances over distillation or export controls could stall negotiations. But experts see room for narrower cooperation. Both countries face threats from non-state actors using AI to sabotage infrastructure, mount cyberattacks, or create biological weapons, argues Alvin W. Graylin, a digital fellow at Stanford’s Digital Economy Lab. Sharing information about those threats could benefit both sides. When Bessent met Chinese Vice-Premier He Lifeng and chief trade negotiator Li Chenggang on Sunday to thrash out summit objectives, he reportedly proposed a formal AI dialogue and a mechanism for each side to notify the other of AI incidents which threaten national security. Modest a measure it may be, it would be an important step towards avoiding some of the worst risks, says Scott Singer, a fellow and co-director of the China AI Initiative at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace. “I wouldn’t take these small but critical steps for granted.” Such a channel could help either side respond during a crisis, and reduce the risk of mistaking a non-state actor or rogue AI as an act of aggression. Reminiscent of the “red phone” that connected Washington to Moscow during the Cold War, experts have floated the idea of a literal fax machine to avoid mistranslations. To be sure, Beijing has not publicly endorsed the proposal. Even if it does, important questions remain. What, for example, would classify a national security threat? Its effectiveness would also depend on whether governments learned of serious incidents. OpenAI only reported its agents' autonomous cyberattack after Hugging Face, the target of the attack, reported the breach to the FBI. Independent researchers have since uncovered other apparent instances of OpenAI agents compromising websites. Beyond the proposed channel, Bessent has said the two countries have agreed to establish a recurring dialogue. Who will staff those talks, and how often they will meet, remains unclear. More than two years elapsed between the last official AI dialogue and Sunday’s meeting. With the stakes quickly rising, Singer says another prolonged hiatus from the negotiating table could prove disastrous. “Who knows where we’re going to be in June 2027,” he says with respect to AI development. “This cannot go off the rails.”

What We Tell Our Kids About AI
2026-09-22

What We Tell Our Kids About AI

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Between us, we have six children. The oldest is 12. None of them has a phone yet, and none has asked us whether artificial intelligence is going to ruin their lives. They will learn that some of the people building the most powerful AI systems have warned that the technology could become dangerous in ways we cannot yet control. They will hear predictions that many of the jobs they might want in the future may disappear. They will encounter arguments about truth, power, safety, and whether our institutions can possibly keep pace with machines advancing faster than our ability to govern them. We will not be able to tell them that any of it is nonsense. We don’t think it is. But another danger looms over this moment. As the conversation around AI grows darker, we risk becoming so consumed by what might go wrong that we stop paying attention to what is beginning to go right. That is not an argument for complacency. The risks are real and demand immediate action. It is an argument for something we think is becoming equally necessary: pragmatic optimism. We work in one of the most failure-prone fields there is, trying to understand disease and determine which medicines might work in human beings. From where we sit, on the front lines of investing in and creating medicines, we are beginning to see AI change what is possible. Drug development has always been a brutal business. Historically, roughly nine out of 10 therapies entering human testing fail. A successful medicine can take a decade or more and billions of dollars to develop. But of course, those statistics obscure the real cost. A failed or missing medicine is a patient still waiting. For most of modern drug development, scientists have started with a hypothesis about biology, searched for a molecule that might affect it, tested that molecule and, overwhelmingly, discovered along the way that they were wrong. AI is beginning to change that story. We have had the wonder of watching this shift in human insight and discovery direction being force-multiplied by AI up close.  We recently watched a team of researchers use AI to design antibodies from scratch against some of the hardest targets in medicine, unlocking approaches that have stymied some of the most sophisticated groups in pharma. Nearby, another group of only five scientists used an AI-driven experimental system to move from an idea for a challenging new medicine to demonstrating what we believe are unique cancer-treating capabilities in a primate in less than six months. Programs with these ambitions would conventionally require teams of specialists working for years. What is changing is not simply the tools available to help us make predictions about human biology. It is the speed of the discovery process itself: propose an idea, build it, test it in the physical world, learn from what happened, and begin again. The scientific method itself is beginning to accelerate, and the number of scientific inquiries, opportunities to advance health per dollar spent, is beginning to scale. Last month, Merck and Moderna reported that a personalized cancer vaccine for melanoma significantly delayed the disease from spreading. The result is remarkable on its own, and studies are already underway in other settings. What we find particularly exciting is how this growing clinical evidence, combined with AI tools and automated labs, can open the field to more competitors. The evidence reduces uncertainty about the approach, while these tools can lower the cost and time required to pursue it. Together, they can bring more teams and more capital into our fight against cancer and expand the number of promising ideas that will be tested at an unprecedented rate. Anyone claiming that AI has solved drug discovery is selling something. Of the 117 AI-enabled drug programs in human trials last year across 63 companies. Not one has been approved. Biology has humbled generations of brilliant scientists, and AI will not repeal biology. Models will be wrong. Drugs will fail. Patients will respond in ways nobody predicted. But we see green shoots and a remarkable new calibration of direction, not a destination. Direction is the whole game in fighting disease and improving human health. AI is beginning to give us better ways of finding it. That is the source of our optimism. Not the belief that the dangers surrounding AI are exaggerated, or that medicine is about to become easy. We believe neither. It comes from watching a technology begin to alter a field in which meaningful progress has historically been painfully slow. Our children will inherit all of this. They will live with the disruptions we are debating now and others none of us has anticipated. They deserve leaders who take those risks seriously and institutions willing to act before every consequence is known, and before some of the most dangerous become irreversible. They also deserve to know what else was happening at this moment. Diseases we could not understand are now yielding more of their secrets. Machines can now search for possibilities no human mind can find alone. Medicines are being designed differently. Diseases too rare or complicated to command sufficient attention are becoming more tractable. And if all of that started to compound, the baseline of human health our children inherit started to be different from the one we inherited ourselves. We don’t yet know what AI will take from our children’s generation. From where we sit, we are beginning to see what it might give. The Past Decade’s Emissions Are Already Driving Worse Heat Waves in Europe
2026-09-22

The Past Decade’s Emissions Are Already Driving Worse Heat Waves in Europe

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A woman shelters from the sun with a parasol on June 21, 2026 in Seville, Spain. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The heat waves Europe has seen in recent years were made measurably more intense because of the greenhouse gasses emitted since the 2015 Paris Climate Agreement, according to a new study published today in Geophysical Research Letters. “We found that the emissions released since the signing of this Paris Agreement in 2015 have substantially and significantly increased the intensity of Europe's recent individual heat waves,” says lead author Jared Trok, a PhD student in Earth system science at the Stanford Doerr School of Sustainability. The study also determined that that held true for Europe’s hottest week in every individual year since at least 2021. Their findings come on the heels of this summer's back-to-back heat waves on the continent, which broke heat records and contributed to at least 35,000 excess deaths. Read more: In Europe, Today's Children Will Live Through Nearly Twice as Many Fire Weather Days Than Their Grandparents The Paris Agreement was a legally binding international treaty on climate change adopted by nearly 200 countries in December 2015, with the aim of limiting global warming to 1.5°C above pre-industrial temperatures. A decade later, the world is expected to blow past that 1.5°C target, with optimistic estimates finding that temperatures will peak at 1.8°C under current climate policies. Beyond the 1.5°C  threshold, every extra fraction of a degree matters. The world can expect to see intensified extreme weather, ecosystem loss, and flooding in coastal cities and island nations, along with far-reaching impacts to human health and food security. Europe is particularly vulnerable in the face of a warming climate. The continent is heating up at more than twice the global average rate. The June and July 2025 heat waves broke records across the continent—only to be surpassed by 2026 heat waves. Surface air temperatures exceeded 40°C (104°F)  in several countries, and up to 46°C (114.8°F) in Spain and Portugal. The researchers created a generative AI model known as a diffusion model using simulated weather data from 10 global climate models to predict how extreme heat waves in Europe could have unfolded under different global emissions levels. “This is a purpose built machine learning model that we trained on large amounts of climate model data…to make predictions about how individual heat waves might have unfolded differently if those same weather patterns had occurred under different levels of human emissions,” says Trok. The team gave the model the observed weather conditions that caused Europe’s most intense annual heat waves from 2016 to 2025, which typically coincided with a persistent, high pressure system known as a heat dome. They had the model predict daily temperatures under emission levels at the time of each extreme heat event, and under the lower emissions levels of 2015. They found that the emissions from the last decade increased the intensity of 2025 heat waves by roughly a third of a degree Celsius. “That third of a degree might seem very small, maybe imperceptible to us walking around, but there's a large body of scientific literature that suggests that even small increments of temperature on a hot day can have these disproportionate impacts on the damages caused by those heat waves,” says Trok. Scientists have long known that the burning of fossil fuels trap greenhouse gases in the atmosphere, raising global temperatures. In recent decades, researchers have developed methods to study how historical emissions have affected heat waves, hurricanes, droughts, wildfires, and other extreme weather events. The researchers say that the methodology represents a step forward for climate modeling. “This research shows that we can systematically and objectively ask questions about subsets of historical emissions, including individual heat waves,” says co-author Noah Diffenbaugh, professor at the Stanford Doerr School of Sustainability. “A key part of what we've identified here is we've been able to say with high confidence that this subset of emissions has shifted the distribution of temperatures that could occur during this individual heat wave to such an extent that we can say we are very confident that this individual heat wave would have been cooler if the same weather patterns had occurred without this subset of emissions.” It also serves as a warning, says Diffenbaugh, noting that the emissions that have already been released will impact our climate regardless of any mitigation efforts we take going forward. “Even in the context of the most optimistic, ambitious future for reducing emissions, there's still more intensification of extremes that is essentially committed.”

Executives of the Year: Ekta Chopra
2026-09-22

Executives of the Year: Ekta Chopra

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In the ever-evolving beauty industry, speed is everything, says Ekta Chopra, chief technology and AI officer at E.l.f. Beauty. “Culture moves at the speed of light,” says Chopra, who assumed her current role in April after celebrating 10 years at the company. “Things go viral on TikTok, and if you don’t capitalize on that very quickly, you lose out.” To help associates work faster and with fewer speed bumps, Chopra is leading efforts to deploy agentic AI across E.l.f.’s enterprise. Her approach is decidedly grassroots. Although she and her team are creating the operating models needed for agents to run smoothly and securely, she’s letting employees decide for themselves where agents can be most helpful. “It’s the people closest to the work who should figure out: What is the work agents can do versus the work humans should do?” she says, adding that the goal is redesigning jobs around innovation instead of administration. “The human is still in the loop … All we’re doing is building infrastructure that helps them react more quickly to opportunities.”
